Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, yeah, it’s nice to meet you too. Um, well, I guess mostly I’m here because I’m just... I’m so incredibly tired. And it’s not just a normal tired, you know? Like, usually if you have a long day, you go to sleep and you wake up and you feel refreshed and ready to go. But for me right now, it’s this deep, heavy exhaustion that sleep just absolutely doesn't fix at all. I could sleep for hours and hours and still wake up feeling like I haven't rested a single minute. 

It’s getting in the way of everything. Like, I’m taking this certificate class right now—just a few courses, I have some college from before but I’m trying to finish this new program up while I’m not working—and just sitting down to read the material for it feels like climbing a mountain. It’s just too much. Even the really small, mundane stuff around the house, like... unloading the dishwasher. I’ll open it and look at the clean dishes, and just the thought of lifting them and putting them away in the cupboards is completely overwhelming. I just don't have the physical or mental energy for it, and it’s been so frustrating because I really want to get things done and get through my coursework, but my body just won't cooperate with me no matter how much rest I try to get.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um... worse? I don't know if "worse" is really the right word for it. It's not like... it's not like I do something and then suddenly I crash harder, if that makes sense? Because the crash is just kind of already there. 

Like, let's take the dishwasher example again. If I actually manage to force myself to put the plates away—which, like I said, is this whole big ordeal where I have to stand in the kitchen and basically negotiate with myself just to pick up a single coffee mug—once I'm actually done with it, I'm still just exactly as exhausted as I was before I started. I don't feel like I've drained a battery, because it feels like the battery was already sitting at zero, you know? I'll go sit back down on the couch and look at my laptop for my classes, and I'm just as tired as I was when I first woke up. 

So it's not like a punishment for doing the activity, it's just this constant, heavy blanket over me the entire time. My mom was actually asking me on the phone the other day if maybe I'm overexerting myself with the coursework, but I really don't think it's that at all. It's just a flatline of being completely drained, no matter if I manage to read a whole chapter or if I just lay in bed all afternoon doing absolutely nothing. It just stays exactly the same level of heavy.

Um... no, not really? I mean, I know it probably sounds like I am, just because I'm talking about not being able to unload the dishwasher or get off the couch. And honestly, my mom—who I talk to pretty much every day, she’s been really checking in on me since I started this program—she actually asked me the exact same thing last week. She was like, are you feeling depressed? And I really had to sit and think about it for a minute, because it’s a fair question when you’re spending this much time in bed. 

But... no. I really don't feel sad. I don't have that, like, heavy emotional darkness or anything like that. I'm not crying for no reason, and I don't feel hopeless about the future. In fact, I'm actually really excited about this certificate. It's something I've wanted to pivot into for a while now since my last job didn't really work out, and the material is actually super interesting to me. So the motivation is definitely there, mentally, you know? Like, my brain *wants* to do the things. I want to read the chapters, I want to have a clean kitchen, I want to be productive. I just... I literally just can't because of the exhaustion. 

So it's not a mood thing, at least I don't think it is. It's just this physical and mental tiredness. Emotionally, I feel pretty fine. Honestly, mostly I'm just really, really frustrated that my body is acting like this when my mood is totally fine and I have so much I actually want to get done. If I was depressed, I feel like I wouldn't even care about the dishes or the classes, but I *do* care, very much, which is why this is driving me so crazy.

Cold? Um, no, not really. I mean, my apartment is pretty drafty, it's this older building and the windows are terrible, so I do wear fuzzy socks and sweaters around the house, but that's just normal for this time of year, you know? Like, I'm not shivering uncontrollably or anything like that. Sick? Um... let me really think back. No, definitely not. I mean, I haven't had a fever or a cough or a sore throat or anything like that in... gosh, it has to be at least a year. 

Actually, it's funny you ask that, because my mom—who, like I said, worries about literally everything—she asked me the exact same thing a couple of weeks ago when I first started complaining about how heavy my body felt. She was convinced I must have caught COVID again or maybe some kind of mono, because I guess post-viral fatigue is a big thing right now? But I haven't even had so much as a sniffle. I'm really careful anyway, and since I'm mostly just at home in my apartment right now doing this certificate, I'm not really out in crowds where I'd even catch a flu. I go to the grocery store, maybe grab a coffee at the drive-thru, but that's really it. 

So no, there was absolutely no viral thing before this started. It literally just sort of... crept up on me out of nowhere. I remember exactly when it started, too, because I had just gotten my syllabus for the new classes and I was totally fine physically. I was actually at my desk getting all my highlighters and notebooks organized, feeling totally normal, and then within a few days it was just this absolute wall of exhaustion. 

It's so frustrating, honestly, because if I *had* been sick, at least this would make sense, you know? I'd be able to tell myself, "Oh, okay, my immune system took a hit, my body is just recovering from a nasty bug, just give it time." But there's no excuse for it! I was perfectly healthy, no flu, no cold, nothing. Just... boom, suddenly I can't even stand up to put a plate in the dishwasher.

I don't feel like I've drained a battery, because it feels like the battery was already sitting at zero, you know? I'll go sit back down on the couch and look at my laptop for my classes, and I'm just as tired as I was when I first woke up. \n\nSo it's not like a punishment for doing the activity, it's just this constant, heavy blanket over me the entire time. My mom was actually asking me on the phone the other day if maybe I'm overexerting myself with the coursework, but I really don't think it's that at all. It's just a flatline of being completely drained, no matter if I manage to read a whole chapter or if I just lay in bed all afternoon doing absolutely nothing. It just stays exactly the same level of heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Um... no, not really? I mean, I know it probably sounds like I am, just because I'm talking about not being able to unload the dishwasher or get off the couch. And honestly, my mom—who I talk to pretty much every day, she’s been really checking in on me since I started this program—she actually asked me the exact same thing last week. She was like, are you feeling depressed? And I really had to sit and think about it for a minute, because it’s a fair question when you’re spending this much time in bed. \n\nBut... no. I really don't feel sad. I don't have that, like, heavy emotional darkness or anything like that. I'm not crying for no reason, and I don't feel hopeless about the future. In fact, I'm actually really excited about this certificate. It's something I've wanted to pivot into for a while now since my last job didn't really work out, and the material is actually super interesting to me. So the motivation is definitely there, mentally, you know? Like, my brain *wants* to do the things. I want to read the chapters, I want to have a clean kitchen, I want to be productive. I just... I literally just can't because of the exhaustion. \n\nSo it's not a mood thing, at least I don't think it is. It's just this physical and mental tiredness. Emotionally, I feel pretty fine. Honestly, mostly I'm just really, really frustrated that my body is acting like this when my mood is totally fine and I have so much I actually want to get done. If I was depressed, I feel like I wouldn't even care about the dishes or the classes, but I *do* care, very much, which is why this is driving me so crazy.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually sensitive to cold?\n\nPatient: Cold? Um, no, not really. I mean, my apartment is pretty drafty, it's this older building and the windows are terrible, so I do wear fuzzy socks and sweaters around the house, but that's just normal for this time of year, you know? Like, I'm not shivering uncontrollably or anything like that. I actually keep the thermostat at like 68 degrees because I hate paying a huge heating bill, especially since I'm not working right now and just doing the certificate program. \n\nSo yeah, I wouldn't say I'm unusually sensitive to it. If anything, I'm just sitting still so much on the couch that my circulation probably isn't great, which is why I put the socks on. But it's not like a bone-chilling cold or anything out of the ordinary for me. It's really just the exhaustion that's the main issue. I don't feel like my temperature regulation is off, just my energy levels. My mom is always telling me I should turn the heat up, but I really don't think that's the problem. I'm just so tired.\n\nDoctor: Did this exhaustion start after you were sick with something like a flu or viral illness?\n\nPatient: Sick? Um... let me really think back. No, definitely not. I mean, I haven't had a fever or a cough or a sore throat or anything like that in... gosh, it has to be at least a year. \n\nActually, it's funny you ask that, because my mom—who, like I said, worries about literally everything—she asked me the exact same thing a couple of weeks ago when I first started complaining about how heavy my body felt. She was convinced I must have caught COVID again or maybe some kind of mono, because I guess post-viral fatigue is a big thing right now? But I haven't even had so much as a sniffle. I'm really careful anyway, and since I'm mostly just at home in my apartment right now doing this certificate, I'm not really out in crowds where I'd even catch a flu. I go to the grocery store, maybe grab a coffee at the drive-thru, but that's really it. \n\nSo no, there was absolutely no viral thing before this started. It literally just sort of... crept up on me out of nowhere. I remember exactly when it started, too, because I had just gotten my syllabus for the new classes and I was totally fine physically. I was actually at my desk getting all my highlighters and notebooks organized, feeling totally normal, and then within a few days it was just this absolute wall of exhaustion. \n\nIt's so frustrating, honestly, because if I *had* been sick, at least this would make sense, you know? I'd be able to tell myself, \"Oh, okay, my immune system took a hit, my body is just recovering from a nasty bug, just give it time.\" But there's no excuse for it! I was perfectly healthy, no flu, no cold, nothing. Just... boom, suddenly I can't even stand up to put a plate in the dishwasher.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Just... boom, suddenly I can't even stand up to put a plate in the dishwasher.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid this start soon after you began any new medication or substance?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 33 / no_occupation / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Medication? Um... no. No, definitely not. I mean, I don't even really like taking Tylenol unless I absolutely have to, you know? Like, if I get a headache, I will usually just drink a huge glass of water and go lie down in a dark room way before I ever reach for a pill bottle. So I definitely haven't started any new prescriptions or anything like that. And I don't really do any recreational stuff or drink either, especially right now because money is so tight since I am not working and just focusing on this certificate program. Going out for drinks just isn't in the budget at all, and honestly, with how utterly exhausted I am, the thought of drinking alcohol or putting anything else in my body just sounds terrible anyway. 

My mom actually asked me the same sort of thing, though. She was wondering if maybe I was taking a new vitamin or some herbal supplement that was messing with my system, because she is always reading these articles online about how unregulated supplements can cause fatigue or liver issues or whatever. She wanted to know if I was taking that ashwagandha stuff again, because I tried that like three years ago for stress when my last job was getting really bad and I was trying to cope. But I told her no, I am literally just taking my standard, generic multivitamin that I have been taking since I was probably twenty-five. I haven't changed brands, I haven't added any extra vitamin D or iron or anything new at all. 

So yeah, no new medications, no new substances, nothing. It is exactly like I was saying before with the whole virus question. If it was a new medication, I feel like I would be so relieved! Because then I could just stop taking it, right? I could just flush it down the toilet and my energy would come back and I could finally get my kitchen clean and actually focus on my coursework. But there is literally nothing I can point to. I am just living my exact normal life, taking the exact same multivitamin, doing nothing different, and yet I still feel like I am trying to move my body through wet cement all day.

I don't feel like I've drained a battery, because it feels like the battery was already sitting at zero, you know? I actually keep the thermostat at like 68 degrees because I hate paying a huge heating bill, especially since I'm not working right now and just doing the certificate program. \n\nSo yeah, I wouldn't say I'm unusually sensitive to it. If anything, I'm just sitting still so much on the couch that my circulation probably isn't great, which is why I put the socks on. But it's not like a bone-chilling cold or anything out of the ordinary for me. It's really just the exhaustion that's the main issue. I don't feel like my temperature regulation is off, just my energy levels. My mom is always telling me I should turn the heat up, but I really don't think that's the problem. I'm just so tired.\n\nDoctor: Did this exhaustion start after you were sick with something like a flu or viral illness?\n\nPatient: Sick? Um... let me really think back. No, definitely not. I mean, I haven't had a fever or a cough or a sore throat or anything like that in... gosh, it has to be at least a year. \n\nActually, it's funny you ask that, because my mom—who, like I said, worries about literally everything—she asked me the exact same thing a couple of weeks ago when I first started complaining about how heavy my body felt. She was convinced I must have caught COVID again or maybe some kind of mono, because I guess post-viral fatigue is a big thing right now? But I haven't even had so much as a sniffle. I'm really careful anyway, and since I'm mostly just at home in my apartment right now doing this certificate, I'm not really out in crowds where I'd even catch a flu. I go to the grocery store, maybe grab a coffee at the drive-thru, but that's really it. \n\nSo no, there was absolutely no viral thing before this started. It literally just sort of... crept up on me out of nowhere. I remember exactly when it started, too, because I had just gotten my syllabus for the new classes and I was totally fine physically. I was actually at my desk getting all my highlighters and notebooks organized, feeling totally normal, and then within a few days it was just this absolute wall of exhaustion. \n\nIt's so frustrating, honestly, because if I *had* been sick, at least this would make sense, you know? I'd be able to tell myself, \"Oh, okay, my immune system took a hit, my body is just recovering from a nasty bug, just give it time.\" But there's no excuse for it! I was perfectly healthy, no flu, no cold, nothing. Just... boom, suddenly I can't even stand up to put a plate in the dishwasher.\n\nDoctor: Did this start soon after you began any new medication or substance?\n\nPatient: Medication? Um... no. No, definitely not. I mean, I don't even really like taking Tylenol unless I absolutely have to, you know? Like, if I get a headache, I will usually just drink a huge glass of water and go lie down in a dark room way before I ever reach for a pill bottle. So I definitely haven't started any new prescriptions or anything like that. And I don't really do any recreational stuff or drink either, especially right now because money is so tight since I am not working and just focusing on this certificate program. Going out for drinks just isn't in the budget at all, and honestly, with how utterly exhausted I am, the thought of drinking alcohol or putting anything else in my body just sounds terrible anyway. \n\nMy mom actually asked me the same sort of thing, though. She was wondering if maybe I was taking a new vitamin or some herbal supplement that was messing with my system, because she is always reading these articles online about how unregulated supplements can cause fatigue or liver issues or whatever. She wanted to know if I was taking that ashwagandha stuff again, because I tried that like three years ago for stress when my last job was getting really bad and I was trying to cope. But I told her no, I am literally just taking my standard, generic multivitamin that I have been taking since I was probably twenty-five. I haven't changed brands, I haven't added any extra vitamin D or iron or anything new at all. \n\nSo yeah, no new medications, no new substances, nothing. It is exactly like I was saying before with the whole virus question. If it was a new medication, I feel like I would be so relieved! Because then I could just stop taking it, right? I could just flush it down the toilet and my energy would come back and I could finally get my kitchen clean and actually focus on my coursework. But there is literally nothing I can point to. I am just living my exact normal life, taking the exact same multivitamin, doing nothing different, and yet I still feel like I am trying to move my body through wet cement all day.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Interest? Yeah. Yeah, actually, that's... that's a huge part of it. It's like someone just flipped a switch and drained all the color out of everything. I just... I don't care anymore. And it's so weird because usually, I'm a person who has a lot of hobbies, you know? Like, things that make me feel like *me*. 

Take my sketching, for example. I usually carry this little Moleskine notebook everywhere—like, in my purse, in my car, just everywhere—and I'm always doodling or doing quick charcoal sketches of people at coffee shops or weird architecture I see. I've done it since high school. But I haven't even opened that notebook in... I don't even know how long. Weeks, definitely. It's just sitting on my nightstand gathering dust. And I usually love going to gallery openings downtown. There's this one collective that does these really cool mixed-media exhibits on the first Friday of every month, and I never miss it. I love the cheap wine, I love talking to the artists, I just love the energy. But the last one came and went, and I didn't even consider going. My friend Sarah texted me to ask if we were meeting up, and I just lied and said I had a headache because the thought of getting dressed and making small talk sounded like actual torture.

It's not just the art stuff, either. It's everything. I usually love cooking. I'm the kind of person who will spend three hours on a Sunday making a really complicated Bolognese sauce from scratch, chopping all the carrots and celery perfectly, letting it simmer all afternoon so the whole apartment smells amazing. Now? I'm literally eating plain toast or microwaving a bowl of frozen peas because I can't even muster the enthusiasm to boil pasta. It's just... what's the point? 

Even my physical outlets are just gone. I usually do this Vinyasa flow yoga class three mornings a week. I love my instructor, Chloe, she has this incredibly soothing voice and the studio has these beautiful floor-to-ceiling windows. I haven't been in almost a month. I keep telling myself I'll go tomorrow, but tomorrow comes and I just stare at my yoga mat in the corner and feel nothing. Nothing at all. Same with tennis. My friend Mark and I usually play at the public courts by the park on Saturday mornings. We're not very good, it's mostly just an excuse to run around and then go get brunch afterward, but it's always fun. He's asked me to play the last three weekends in a row, and I just keep making excuses. I told him my knee was acting up, which is a total lie. I just... I don't want to do it. I don't want to do *any* of it. It's like the part of my brain that feels joy or excitement or even just mild interest is completely broken right now.

Like, I’m taking this certificate class right now—just a few courses, I have some college from before but I’m trying to finish this new program up while I’m not working—and just sitting down to read the material for it feels like climbing a mountain. It’s just too much. Even the really small, mundane stuff around the house, like... unloading the dishwasher. I’ll open it and look at the clean dishes, and just the thought of lifting them and putting them away in the cupboards is completely overwhelming. I just don't have the physical or mental energy for it, and it’s been so frustrating because I really want to get things done and get through my coursework, but my body just won't cooperate with me no matter how much rest I try to get.\n\nDoctor: After even small amounts of physical or mental activity, do you feel markedly worse afterward?\n\nPatient: Um... worse? I don't know if \"worse\" is really the right word for it. It's not like... it's not like I do something and then suddenly I crash harder, if that makes sense? Because the crash is just kind of already there. \n\nLike, let's take the dishwasher example again. If I actually manage to force myself to put the plates away—which, like I said, is this whole big ordeal where I have to stand in the kitchen and basically negotiate with myself just to pick up a single coffee mug—once I'm actually done with it, I'm still just exactly as exhausted as I was before I started. I don't feel like I've drained a battery, because it feels like the battery was already sitting at zero, you know? I'll go sit back down on the couch and look at my laptop for my classes, and I'm just as tired as I was when I first woke up. \n\nSo it's not like a punishment for doing the activity, it's just this constant, heavy blanket over me the entire time. My mom was actually asking me on the phone the other day if maybe I'm overexerting myself with the coursework, but I really don't think it's that at all. It's just a flatline of being completely drained, no matter if I manage to read a whole chapter or if I just lay in bed all afternoon doing absolutely nothing. It just stays exactly the same level of heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Um... no, not really? I mean, I know it probably sounds like I am, just because I'm talking about not being able to unload the dishwasher or get off the couch. And honestly, my mom—who I talk to pretty much every day, she’s been really checking in on me since I started this program—she actually asked me the exact same thing last week. She was like, are you feeling depressed? And I really had to sit and think about it for a minute, because it’s a fair question when you’re spending this much time in bed. \n\nBut... no. I really don't feel sad. I don't have that, like, heavy emotional darkness or anything like that. I'm not crying for no reason, and I don't feel hopeless about the future. In fact, I'm actually really excited about this certificate. It's something I've wanted to pivot into for a while now since my last job didn't really work out, and the material is actually super interesting to me. So the motivation is definitely there, mentally, you know? Like, my brain *wants* to do the things. I want to read the chapters, I want to have a clean kitchen, I want to be productive. I just... I literally just can't because of the exhaustion. \n\nSo it's not a mood thing, at least I don't think it is. It's just this physical and mental tiredness. Emotionally, I feel pretty fine. Honestly, mostly I'm just really, really frustrated that my body is acting like this when my mood is totally fine and I have so much I actually want to get done. If I was depressed, I feel like I wouldn't even care about the dishes or the classes, but I *do* care, very much, which is why this is driving me so crazy.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually sensitive to cold?\n\nPatient: Cold? Um, no, not really. I mean, my apartment is pretty drafty, it's this older building and the windows are terrible, so I do wear fuzzy socks and sweaters around the house, but that's just normal for this time of year, you know? Like, I'm not shivering uncontrollably or anything like that. I actually keep the thermostat at like 68 degrees because I hate paying a huge heating bill, especially since I'm not working right now and just doing the certificate program. \n\nSo yeah, I wouldn't say I'm unusually sensitive to it. If anything, I'm just sitting still so much on the couch that my circulation probably isn't great, which is why I put the socks on. But it's not like a bone-chilling cold or anything out of the ordinary for me. It's really just the exhaustion that's the main issue. I don't feel like my temperature regulation is off, just my energy levels. My mom is always telling me I should turn the heat up, but I really don't think that's the problem. I'm just so tired.\n\nDoctor: Did this exhaustion start after you were sick with something like a flu or viral illness?\n\nPatient: Sick? Um... let me really think back. No, definitely not. I mean, I haven't had a fever or a cough or a sore throat or anything like that in... gosh, it has to be at least a year. \n\nActually, it's funny you ask that, because my mom—who, like I said, worries about literally everything—she asked me the exact same thing a couple of weeks ago when I first started complaining about how heavy my body felt. She was convinced I must have caught COVID again or maybe some kind of mono, because I guess post-viral fatigue is a big thing right now? But I haven't even had so much as a sniffle. I'm really careful anyway, and since I'm mostly just at home in my apartment right now doing this certificate, I'm not really out in crowds where I'd even catch a flu. I go to the grocery store, maybe grab a coffee at the drive-thru, but that's really it. \n\nSo no, there was absolutely no viral thing before this started. It literally just sort of... crept up on me out of nowhere. I remember exactly when it started, too, because I had just gotten my syllabus for the new classes and I was totally fine physically. I was actually at my desk getting all my highlighters and notebooks organized, feeling totally normal, and then within a few days it was just this absolute wall of exhaustion. \n\nIt's so frustrating, honestly, because if I *had* been sick, at least this would make sense, you know? I'd be able to tell myself, \"Oh, okay, my immune system took a hit, my body is just recovering from a nasty bug, just give it time.\" But there's no excuse for it! I was perfectly healthy, no flu, no cold, nothing. Just... boom, suddenly I can't even stand up to put a plate in the dishwasher.\n\nDoctor: Did this start soon after you began any new medication or substance?\n\nPatient: Medication? Um... no. No, definitely not. I mean, I don't even really like taking Tylenol unless I absolutely have to, you know? Like, if I get a headache, I will usually just drink a huge glass of water and go lie down in a dark room way before I ever reach for a pill bottle. So I definitely haven't started any new prescriptions or anything like that. And I don't really do any recreational stuff or drink either, especially right now because money is so tight since I am not working and just focusing on this certificate program. Going out for drinks just isn't in the budget at all, and honestly, with how utterly exhausted I am, the thought of drinking alcohol or putting anything else in my body just sounds terrible anyway. \n\nMy mom actually asked me the same sort of thing, though. She was wondering if maybe I was taking a new vitamin or some herbal supplement that was messing with my system, because she is always reading these articles online about how unregulated supplements can cause fatigue or liver issues or whatever. She wanted to know if I was taking that ashwagandha stuff again, because I tried that like three years ago for stress when my last job was getting really bad and I was trying to cope. But I told her no, I am literally just taking my standard, generic multivitamin that I have been taking since I was probably twenty-five. I haven't changed brands, I haven't added any extra vitamin D or iron or anything new at all. \n\nSo yeah, no new medications, no new substances, nothing. It is exactly like I was saying before with the whole virus question. If it was a new medication, I feel like I would be so relieved! Because then I could just stop taking it, right? I could just flush it down the toilet and my energy would come back and I could finally get my kitchen clean and actually focus on my coursework. But there is literally nothing I can point to. I am just living my exact normal life, taking the exact same multivitamin, doing nothing different, and yet I still feel like I am trying to move my body through wet cement all day.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Interest? Yeah. Yeah, actually, that's... that's a huge part of it. It's like someone just flipped a switch and drained all the color out of everything. I just... I don't care anymore. And it's so weird because usually, I'm a person who has a lot of hobbies, you know? Like, things that make me feel like *me*. \n\nTake my sketching, for example. I usually carry this little Moleskine notebook everywhere—like, in my purse, in my car, just everywhere—and I'm always doodling or doing quick charcoal sketches of people at coffee shops or weird architecture I see. I've done it since high school. But I haven't even opened that notebook in... I don't even know how long. Weeks, definitely. It's just sitting on my nightstand gathering dust. And I usually love going to gallery openings downtown. There's this one collective that does these really cool mixed-media exhibits on the first Friday of every month, and I never miss it. I love the cheap wine, I love talking to the artists, I just love the energy. But the last one came and went, and I didn't even consider going. My friend Sarah texted me to ask if we were meeting up, and I just lied and said I had a headache because the thought of getting dressed and making small talk sounded like actual torture.\n\nIt's not just the art stuff, either. It's everything. I usually love cooking. I'm the kind of person who will spend three hours on a Sunday making a really complicated Bolognese sauce from scratch, chopping all the carrots and celery perfectly, letting it simmer all afternoon so the whole apartment smells amazing. Now? I'm literally eating plain toast or microwaving a bowl of frozen peas because I can't even muster the enthusiasm to boil pasta. It's just... what's the point? \n\nEven my physical outlets are just gone. I usually do this Vinyasa flow yoga class three mornings a week. I love my instructor, Chloe, she has this incredibly soothing voice and the studio has these beautiful floor-to-ceiling windows. I haven't been in almost a month. I keep telling myself I'll go tomorrow, but tomorrow comes and I just stare at my yoga mat in the corner and feel nothing. Nothing at all. Same with tennis. My friend Mark and I usually play at the public courts by the park on Saturday mornings. We're not very good, it's mostly just an excuse to run around and then go get brunch afterward, but it's always fun. He's asked me to play the last three weekends in a row, and I just keep making excuses. I told him my knee was acting up, which is a total lie. I just... I don't want to do it. I don't want to do *any* of it. It's like the part of my brain that feels joy or excitement or even just mild interest is completely broken right now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I keep telling myself I'll go tomorrow, but tomorrow comes and I just stare at my yoga mat in the corner and feel nothing. Nothing at all. Same with tennis. My friend Mark and I usually play at the public courts by the park on Saturday mornings. We're not very good, it's mostly just an excuse to run around and then go get brunch afterward, but it's always fun. He's asked me to play the last three weekends in a row, and I just keep making excuses. I told him my knee was acting up, which is a total lie. I just... I don't want to do it. I don't want to do *any* of it. It's like the part of my brain that feels joy or excitement or even just mild interest is completely broken right now.\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore this current period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt this same kind of loss of interest and low energy for an extended stretch?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 33 / no_occupation / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No... no, never. I mean, honestly, this is completely new for me. 

Like I mentioned earlier about that old job I had a few years ago? The one where I was so stressed I tried that ashwagandha supplement? Even then, when I was working ridiculous hours and my boss was just constantly breathing down my neck and I felt totally burned out... I still wanted to do things. I would come home exhausted, sure, from dealing with people all day, but I would still immediately want to go to my yoga class to decompress, or I'd still text Sarah to see if she wanted to grab a coffee so I could vent about my day. I never lost my drive to actually *live* my life, you know? 

I've always been one of those people who kind of runs on a higher gear. My mom used to joke that I was like a shark, like if I stopped moving I would die. I was always the one organizing the weekend plans, or trying out a new recipe, or dragging Mark to the tennis courts even if it was slightly drizzling. I just always had this baseline of energy and curiosity about things. 

So to just have this... this heavy, gray blanket over everything now? Where I can't even get off the couch to sketch or make toast? No, I've never experienced anything even remotely close to this in my entire life. It's honestly kind of scaring me, to be perfectly honest with you, because it is just so wildly out of character. If I had felt like this before, maybe I'd know how to handle it or have some kind of coping mechanism, or I could at least tell myself that it passes eventually, but I'm just totally lost right now because this is literally the first time my brain and my body have ever just... shut down like this.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F33.9, F48.0, F06.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, yeah, it’s nice to meet you too. Um, well, I guess mostly I’m here because I’m just... I’m so incredibly tired. And it’s not just a normal tired, you know? Like, usually if you have a long day, you go to sleep and you wake up and you feel refreshed and ready to go. But for me right now, it’s this deep, heavy exhaustion that sleep just absolutely doesn't fix at all. I could sleep for hours and hours and still wake up feeling like I haven't rested a single minute. \n\nIt’s getting in the way of everything. Like, I’m taking this certificate class right now—just a few courses, I have some college from before but I’m trying to finish this new program up while I’m not working—and just sitting down to read the material for it feels like climbing a mountain. It’s just too much. Even the really small, mundane stuff around the house, like... unloading the dishwasher. I’ll open it and look at the clean dishes, and just the thought of lifting them and putting them away in the cupboards is completely overwhelming. I just don't have the physical or mental energy for it, and it’s been so frustrating because I really want to get things done and get through my coursework, but my body just won't cooperate with me no matter how much rest I try to get.\n\nDoctor: After even small amounts of physical or mental activity, do you feel markedly worse afterward?\n\nPatient: Um... worse? I don't know if \"worse\" is really the right word for it. It's not like... it's not like I do something and then suddenly I crash harder, if that makes sense? Because the crash is just kind of already there. \n\nLike, let's take the dishwasher example again. If I actually manage to force myself to put the plates away—which, like I said, is this whole big ordeal where I have to stand in the kitchen and basically negotiate with myself just to pick up a single coffee mug—once I'm actually done with it, I'm still just exactly as exhausted as I was before I started. I don't feel like I've drained a battery, because it feels like the battery was already sitting at zero, you know? I'll go sit back down on the couch and look at my laptop for my classes, and I'm just as tired as I was when I first woke up. \n\nSo it's not like a punishment for doing the activity, it's just this constant, heavy blanket over me the entire time. My mom was actually asking me on the phone the other day if maybe I'm overexerting myself with the coursework, but I really don't think it's that at all. It's just a flatline of being completely drained, no matter if I manage to read a whole chapter or if I just lay in bed all afternoon doing absolutely nothing. It just stays exactly the same level of heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Um... no, not really? I mean, I know it probably sounds like I am, just because I'm talking about not being able to unload the dishwasher or get off the couch. And honestly, my mom—who I talk to pretty much every day, she’s been really checking in on me since I started this program—she actually asked me the exact same thing last week. She was like, are you feeling depressed? And I really had to sit and think about it for a minute, because it’s a fair question when you’re spending this much time in bed. \n\nBut... no. I really don't feel sad. I don't have that, like, heavy emotional darkness or anything like that. I'm not crying for no reason, and I don't feel hopeless about the future. In fact, I'm actually really excited about this certificate. It's something I've wanted to pivot into for a while now since my last job didn't really work out, and the material is actually super interesting to me. So the motivation is definitely there, mentally, you know? Like, my brain *wants* to do the things. I want to read the chapters, I want to have a clean kitchen, I want to be productive. I just... I literally just can't because of the exhaustion. \n\nSo it's not a mood thing, at least I don't think it is. It's just this physical and mental tiredness. Emotionally, I feel pretty fine. Honestly, mostly I'm just really, really frustrated that my body is acting like this when my mood is totally fine and I have so much I actually want to get done. If I was depressed, I feel like I wouldn't even care about the dishes or the classes, but I *do* care, very much, which is why this is driving me so crazy.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually sensitive to cold?\n\nPatient: Cold? Um, no, not really. I mean, my apartment is pretty drafty, it's this older building and the windows are terrible, so I do wear fuzzy socks and sweaters around the house, but that's just normal for this time of year, you know? Like, I'm not shivering uncontrollably or anything like that. I actually keep the thermostat at like 68 degrees because I hate paying a huge heating bill, especially since I'm not working right now and just doing the certificate program. \n\nSo yeah, I wouldn't say I'm unusually sensitive to it. If anything, I'm just sitting still so much on the couch that my circulation probably isn't great, which is why I put the socks on. But it's not like a bone-chilling cold or anything out of the ordinary for me. It's really just the exhaustion that's the main issue. I don't feel like my temperature regulation is off, just my energy levels. My mom is always telling me I should turn the heat up, but I really don't think that's the problem. I'm just so tired.\n\nDoctor: Did this exhaustion start after you were sick with something like a flu or viral illness?\n\nPatient: Sick? Um... let me really think back. No, definitely not. I mean, I haven't had a fever or a cough or a sore throat or anything like that in... gosh, it has to be at least a year. \n\nActually, it's funny you ask that, because my mom—who, like I said, worries about literally everything—she asked me the exact same thing a couple of weeks ago when I first started complaining about how heavy my body felt. She was convinced I must have caught COVID again or maybe some kind of mono, because I guess post-viral fatigue is a big thing right now? But I haven't even had so much as a sniffle. I'm really careful anyway, and since I'm mostly just at home in my apartment right now doing this certificate, I'm not really out in crowds where I'd even catch a flu. I go to the grocery store, maybe grab a coffee at the drive-thru, but that's really it. \n\nSo no, there was absolutely no viral thing before this started. It literally just sort of... crept up on me out of nowhere. I remember exactly when it started, too, because I had just gotten my syllabus for the new classes and I was totally fine physically. I was actually at my desk getting all my highlighters and notebooks organized, feeling totally normal, and then within a few days it was just this absolute wall of exhaustion. \n\nIt's so frustrating, honestly, because if I *had* been sick, at least this would make sense, you know? I'd be able to tell myself, \"Oh, okay, my immune system took a hit, my body is just recovering from a nasty bug, just give it time.\" But there's no excuse for it! I was perfectly healthy, no flu, no cold, nothing. Just... boom, suddenly I can't even stand up to put a plate in the dishwasher.\n\nDoctor: Did this start soon after you began any new medication or substance?\n\nPatient: Medication? Um... no. No, definitely not. I mean, I don't even really like taking Tylenol unless I absolutely have to, you know? Like, if I get a headache, I will usually just drink a huge glass of water and go lie down in a dark room way before I ever reach for a pill bottle. So I definitely haven't started any new prescriptions or anything like that. And I don't really do any recreational stuff or drink either, especially right now because money is so tight since I am not working and just focusing on this certificate program. Going out for drinks just isn't in the budget at all, and honestly, with how utterly exhausted I am, the thought of drinking alcohol or putting anything else in my body just sounds terrible anyway. \n\nMy mom actually asked me the same sort of thing, though. She was wondering if maybe I was taking a new vitamin or some herbal supplement that was messing with my system, because she is always reading these articles online about how unregulated supplements can cause fatigue or liver issues or whatever. She wanted to know if I was taking that ashwagandha stuff again, because I tried that like three years ago for stress when my last job was getting really bad and I was trying to cope. But I told her no, I am literally just taking my standard, generic multivitamin that I have been taking since I was probably twenty-five. I haven't changed brands, I haven't added any extra vitamin D or iron or anything new at all. \n\nSo yeah, no new medications, no new substances, nothing. It is exactly like I was saying before with the whole virus question. If it was a new medication, I feel like I would be so relieved! Because then I could just stop taking it, right? I could just flush it down the toilet and my energy would come back and I could finally get my kitchen clean and actually focus on my coursework. But there is literally nothing I can point to. I am just living my exact normal life, taking the exact same multivitamin, doing nothing different, and yet I still feel like I am trying to move my body through wet cement all day.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Interest? Yeah. Yeah, actually, that's... that's a huge part of it. It's like someone just flipped a switch and drained all the color out of everything. I just... I don't care anymore. And it's so weird because usually, I'm a person who has a lot of hobbies, you know? Like, things that make me feel like *me*. \n\nTake my sketching, for example. I usually carry this little Moleskine notebook everywhere—like, in my purse, in my car, just everywhere—and I'm always doodling or doing quick charcoal sketches of people at coffee shops or weird architecture I see. I've done it since high school. But I haven't even opened that notebook in... I don't even know how long. Weeks, definitely. It's just sitting on my nightstand gathering dust. And I usually love going to gallery openings downtown. There's this one collective that does these really cool mixed-media exhibits on the first Friday of every month, and I never miss it. I love the cheap wine, I love talking to the artists, I just love the energy. But the last one came and went, and I didn't even consider going. My friend Sarah texted me to ask if we were meeting up, and I just lied and said I had a headache because the thought of getting dressed and making small talk sounded like actual torture.\n\nIt's not just the art stuff, either. It's everything. I usually love cooking. I'm the kind of person who will spend three hours on a Sunday making a really complicated Bolognese sauce from scratch, chopping all the carrots and celery perfectly, letting it simmer all afternoon so the whole apartment smells amazing. Now? I'm literally eating plain toast or microwaving a bowl of frozen peas because I can't even muster the enthusiasm to boil pasta. It's just... what's the point? \n\nEven my physical outlets are just gone. I usually do this Vinyasa flow yoga class three mornings a week. My friend Mark and I usually play at the public courts by the park on Saturday mornings. We're not very good, it's mostly just an excuse to run around and then go get brunch afterward, but it's always fun. He's asked me to play the last three weekends in a row, and I just keep making excuses. I told him my knee was acting up, which is a total lie. I just... I don't want to do it. I don't want to do *any* of it. It's like the part of my brain that feels joy or excitement or even just mild interest is completely broken right now.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt this same kind of loss of interest and low energy for an extended stretch?\n\nPatient: No... no, never. I mean, honestly, this is completely new for me. \n\nLike I mentioned earlier about that old job I had a few years ago? The one where I was so stressed I tried that ashwagandha supplement? I'm the kind of person who will spend three hours on a Sunday making a really complicated Bolognese sauce from scratch, chopping all the carrots and celery perfectly, letting it simmer all afternoon so the whole apartment smells amazing. Now? I'm literally eating plain toast or microwaving a bowl of frozen peas because I can't even muster the enthusiasm to boil pasta. It's just... what's the point? \n\nEven my physical outlets are just gone. I usually do this Vinyasa flow yoga class three mornings a week. I keep telling myself I'll go tomorrow, but tomorrow comes and I just stare at my yoga mat in the corner and feel nothing. Nothing at all. Same with tennis. My friend Mark and I usually play at the public courts by the park on Saturday mornings. We're not very good, it's mostly just an excuse to run around and then go get brunch afterward, but it's always fun. He's asked me to play the last three weekends in a row, and I just keep making excuses. I told him my knee was acting up, which is a total lie. I just... I don't want to do it. I don't want to do *any* of it. It's like the part of my brain that feels joy or excitement or even just mild interest is completely broken right now.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt this same kind of loss of interest and low energy for an extended stretch?\n\nPatient: No... no, never. I mean, honestly, this is completely new for me. \n\nLike I mentioned earlier about that old job I had a few years ago? The one where I was so stressed I tried that ashwagandha supplement? Even then, when I was working ridiculous hours and my boss was just constantly breathing down my neck and I felt totally burned out... I still wanted to do things. I would come home exhausted, sure, from dealing with people all day, but I would still immediately want to go to my yoga class to decompress, or I'd still text Sarah to see if she wanted to grab a coffee so I could vent about my day. I never lost my drive to actually *live* my life, you know? \n\nI've always been one of those people who kind of runs on a higher gear. My mom used to joke that I was like a shark, like if I stopped moving I would die. Sad? Um... you know, that's a really interesting question, because my mom actually asked me the exact same thing when I told her I hadn't left the apartment in a couple of days. But no, I wouldn't say I feel sad. Not at all, really. Which I know probably sounds confusing because of everything I just said about not doing my art or going to yoga, but it's true. 

When I think of being sad, I think of, like... crying, you know? Like feeling that heavy ache in your chest and wanting to just weep. Like when I went through this really terrible breakup with my ex-boyfriend, David, back in my twenties. I was devastated. I was crying into my pillow every night, listening to miserable music, calling my friends to sob on the phone, eating my weight in takeout... *that* was sad. I felt things very intensely. 

But this isn't like that at all. I haven't shed a single tear this whole time. Sarah even texted me asking if I needed a shoulder to cry on since I've been so MIA, and I had to tell her, 'Sarah, I'm literally not crying.' I almost wish I *could* cry, to be honest with you. Because at least when you cry, it feels like a release, right? Like you're letting something out, like a pressure valve, and then you can take a deep breath and feel a little lighter afterward. 

But with this, there's nothing to let out. It's not an emotion. It's more like... like a total absence of emotion. I just sit there on the couch and watch the sunlight move across the living room floor over the course of the afternoon, and I don't feel sad, I don't feel happy, I don't feel down... I just feel completely blank. It's just a void. So, no, I definitely wouldn't use the word sad to describe it. It's just that flat, heavy nothingness I was talking about before.

Like, usually if you have a long day, you go to sleep and you wake up and you feel refreshed and ready to go. But for me right now, it’s this deep, heavy exhaustion that sleep just absolutely doesn't fix at all. I could sleep for hours and hours and still wake up feeling like I haven't rested a single minute. \n\nIt’s getting in the way of everything. Like, I’m taking this certificate class right now—just a few courses, I have some college from before but I’m trying to finish this new program up while I’m not working—and just sitting down to read the material for it feels like climbing a mountain. It’s just too much. Even the really small, mundane stuff around the house, like... unloading the dishwasher. I’ll open it and look at the clean dishes, and just the thought of lifting them and putting them away in the cupboards is completely overwhelming. I just don't have the physical or mental energy for it, and it’s been so frustrating because I really want to get things done and get through my coursework, but my body just won't cooperate with me no matter how much rest I try to get.\n\nDoctor: After even small amounts of physical or mental activity, do you feel markedly worse afterward?\n\nPatient: Um... worse? I don't know if \"worse\" is really the right word for it. It's not like... it's not like I do something and then suddenly I crash harder, if that makes sense? Because the crash is just kind of already there. \n\nLike, let's take the dishwasher example again. If I actually manage to force myself to put the plates away—which, like I said, is this whole big ordeal where I have to stand in the kitchen and basically negotiate with myself just to pick up a single coffee mug—once I'm actually done with it, I'm still just exactly as exhausted as I was before I started. I don't feel like I've drained a battery, because it feels like the battery was already sitting at zero, you know? I'll go sit back down on the couch and look at my laptop for my classes, and I'm just as tired as I was when I first woke up. \n\nSo it's not like a punishment for doing the activity, it's just this constant, heavy blanket over me the entire time. My mom was actually asking me on the phone the other day if maybe I'm overexerting myself with the coursework, but I really don't think it's that at all. It's just a flatline of being completely drained, no matter if I manage to read a whole chapter or if I just lay in bed all afternoon doing absolutely nothing. It just stays exactly the same level of heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Um... no, not really? I mean, I know it probably sounds like I am, just because I'm talking about not being able to unload the dishwasher or get off the couch. And honestly, my mom—who I talk to pretty much every day, she’s been really checking in on me since I started this program—she actually asked me the exact same thing last week. She was like, are you feeling depressed? And I really had to sit and think about it for a minute, because it’s a fair question when you’re spending this much time in bed. \n\nBut... no. I really don't feel sad. I don't have that, like, heavy emotional darkness or anything like that. I'm not crying for no reason, and I don't feel hopeless about the future. In fact, I'm actually really excited about this certificate. It's something I've wanted to pivot into for a while now since my last job didn't really work out, and the material is actually super interesting to me. So the motivation is definitely there, mentally, you know? Like, my brain *wants* to do the things. I want to read the chapters, I want to have a clean kitchen, I want to be productive. I just... I literally just can't because of the exhaustion. \n\nSo it's not a mood thing, at least I don't think it is. It's just this physical and mental tiredness. Emotionally, I feel pretty fine. Honestly, mostly I'm just really, really frustrated that my body is acting like this when my mood is totally fine and I have so much I actually want to get done. If I was depressed, I feel like I wouldn't even care about the dishes or the classes, but I *do* care, very much, which is why this is driving me so crazy.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually sensitive to cold?\n\nPatient: Cold? Um, no, not really. I mean, my apartment is pretty drafty, it's this older building and the windows are terrible, so I do wear fuzzy socks and sweaters around the house, but that's just normal for this time of year, you know? Like, I'm not shivering uncontrollably or anything like that. I actually keep the thermostat at like 68 degrees because I hate paying a huge heating bill, especially since I'm not working right now and just doing the certificate program. \n\nSo yeah, I wouldn't say I'm unusually sensitive to it. If anything, I'm just sitting still so much on the couch that my circulation probably isn't great, which is why I put the socks on. But it's not like a bone-chilling cold or anything out of the ordinary for me. It's really just the exhaustion that's the main issue. I don't feel like my temperature regulation is off, just my energy levels. My mom is always telling me I should turn the heat up, but I really don't think that's the problem. I'm just so tired.\n\nDoctor: Did this exhaustion start after you were sick with something like a flu or viral illness?\n\nPatient: Sick? Um... let me really think back. No, definitely not. I mean, I haven't had a fever or a cough or a sore throat or anything like that in... gosh, it has to be at least a year. \n\nActually, it's funny you ask that, because my mom—who, like I said, worries about literally everything—she asked me the exact same thing a couple of weeks ago when I first started complaining about how heavy my body felt. She was convinced I must have caught COVID again or maybe some kind of mono, because I guess post-viral fatigue is a big thing right now? But I haven't even had so much as a sniffle. I'm really careful anyway, and since I'm mostly just at home in my apartment right now doing this certificate, I'm not really out in crowds where I'd even catch a flu. I go to the grocery store, maybe grab a coffee at the drive-thru, but that's really it. \n\nSo no, there was absolutely no viral thing before this started. It literally just sort of... crept up on me out of nowhere. I remember exactly when it started, too, because I had just gotten my syllabus for the new classes and I was totally fine physically. I was actually at my desk getting all my highlighters and notebooks organized, feeling totally normal, and then within a few days it was just this absolute wall of exhaustion. \n\nIt's so frustrating, honestly, because if I *had* been sick, at least this would make sense, you know? I'd be able to tell myself, \"Oh, okay, my immune system took a hit, my body is just recovering from a nasty bug, just give it time.\" But there's no excuse for it! I was perfectly healthy, no flu, no cold, nothing. Just... boom, suddenly I can't even stand up to put a plate in the dishwasher.\n\nDoctor: Did this start soon after you began any new medication or substance?\n\nPatient: Medication? Um... no. No, definitely not. I mean, I don't even really like taking Tylenol unless I absolutely have to, you know? Like, if I get a headache, I will usually just drink a huge glass of water and go lie down in a dark room way before I ever reach for a pill bottle. So I definitely haven't started any new prescriptions or anything like that. And I don't really do any recreational stuff or drink either, especially right now because money is so tight since I am not working and just focusing on this certificate program. Going out for drinks just isn't in the budget at all, and honestly, with how utterly exhausted I am, the thought of drinking alcohol or putting anything else in my body just sounds terrible anyway. \n\nMy mom actually asked me the same sort of thing, though. She was wondering if maybe I was taking a new vitamin or some herbal supplement that was messing with my system, because she is always reading these articles online about how unregulated supplements can cause fatigue or liver issues or whatever. She wanted to know if I was taking that ashwagandha stuff again, because I tried that like three years ago for stress when my last job was getting really bad and I was trying to cope. But I told her no, I am literally just taking my standard, generic multivitamin that I have been taking since I was probably twenty-five. I haven't changed brands, I haven't added any extra vitamin D or iron or anything new at all. \n\nSo yeah, no new medications, no new substances, nothing. It is exactly like I was saying before with the whole virus question. If it was a new medication, I feel like I would be so relieved! Because then I could just stop taking it, right? I could just flush it down the toilet and my energy would come back and I could finally get my kitchen clean and actually focus on my coursework. But there is literally nothing I can point to. I am just living my exact normal life, taking the exact same multivitamin, doing nothing different, and yet I still feel like I am trying to move my body through wet cement all day.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Interest? Yeah. Yeah, actually, that's... that's a huge part of it. It's like someone just flipped a switch and drained all the color out of everything. I just... I don't care anymore. And it's so weird because usually, I'm a person who has a lot of hobbies, you know? Like, things that make me feel like *me*. \n\nTake my sketching, for example. I usually carry this little Moleskine notebook everywhere—like, in my purse, in my car, just everywhere—and I'm always doodling or doing quick charcoal sketches of people at coffee shops or weird architecture I see. I've done it since high school. But I haven't even opened that notebook in... I don't even know how long. Weeks, definitely. It's just sitting on my nightstand gathering dust. And I usually love going to gallery openings downtown. There's this one collective that does these really cool mixed-media exhibits on the first Friday of every month, and I never miss it. I love the cheap wine, I love talking to the artists, I just love the energy. But the last one came and went, and I didn't even consider going. My friend Sarah texted me to ask if we were meeting up, and I just lied and said I had a headache because the thought of getting dressed and making small talk sounded like actual torture.\n\nIt's not just the art stuff, either. It's everything. I usually love cooking. I'm the kind of person who will spend three hours on a Sunday making a really complicated Bolognese sauce from scratch, chopping all the carrots and celery perfectly, letting it simmer all afternoon so the whole apartment smells amazing. Now? I'm literally eating plain toast or microwaving a bowl of frozen peas because I can't even muster the enthusiasm to boil pasta. It's just... what's the point? \n\nEven my physical outlets are just gone. I usually do this Vinyasa flow yoga class three mornings a week. I love my instructor, Chloe, she has this incredibly soothing voice and the studio has these beautiful floor-to-ceiling windows. I haven't been in almost a month. I keep telling myself I'll go tomorrow, but tomorrow comes and I just stare at my yoga mat in the corner and feel nothing. Nothing at all. Same with tennis. My friend Mark and I usually play at the public courts by the park on Saturday mornings. We're not very good, it's mostly just an excuse to run around and then go get brunch afterward, but it's always fun. He's asked me to play the last three weekends in a row, and I just keep making excuses. I told him my knee was acting up, which is a total lie. I just... I don't want to do it. I don't want to do *any* of it. It's like the part of my brain that feels joy or excitement or even just mild interest is completely broken right now.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt this same kind of loss of interest and low energy for an extended stretch?\n\nPatient: No... no, never. I mean, honestly, this is completely new for me. \n\nLike I mentioned earlier about that old job I had a few years ago? The one where I was so stressed I tried that ashwagandha supplement? Even then, when I was working ridiculous hours and my boss was just constantly breathing down my neck and I felt totally burned out... I still wanted to do things. I would come home exhausted, sure, from dealing with people all day, but I would still immediately want to go to my yoga class to decompress, or I'd still text Sarah to see if she wanted to grab a coffee so I could vent about my day. I never lost my drive to actually *live* my life, you know? \n\nI've always been one of those people who kind of runs on a higher gear. My mom used to joke that I was like a shark, like if I stopped moving I would die. I was always the one organizing the weekend plans, or trying out a new recipe, or dragging Mark to the tennis courts even if it was slightly drizzling. I just always had this baseline of energy and curiosity about things. \n\nSo to just have this... this heavy, gray blanket over everything now? Where I can't even get off the couch to sketch or make toast? No, I've never experienced anything even remotely close to this in my entire life. It's honestly kind of scaring me, to be perfectly honest with you, because it is just so wildly out of character. If I had felt like this before, maybe I'd know how to handle it or have some kind of coping mechanism, or I could at least tell myself that it passes eventually, but I'm just totally lost right now because this is literally the first time my brain and my body have ever just... shut down like this.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you been feeling sad or down most of the day?\n\nPatient: Sad? Um... you know, that's a really interesting question, because my mom actually asked me the exact same thing when I told her I hadn't left the apartment in a couple of days. But no, I wouldn't say I feel sad. Not at all, really. Which I know probably sounds confusing because of everything I just said about not doing my art or going to yoga, but it's true. \n\nWhen I think of being sad, I think of, like... crying, you know? Like feeling that heavy ache in your chest and wanting to just weep. Like when I went through this really terrible breakup with my ex-boyfriend, David, back in my twenties. I was devastated. I was crying into my pillow every night, listening to miserable music, calling my friends to sob on the phone, eating my weight in takeout... *that* was sad. I felt things very intensely. \n\nBut this isn't like that at all. I haven't shed a single tear this whole time. Sarah even texted me asking if I needed a shoulder to cry on since I've been so MIA, and I had to tell her, 'Sarah, I'm literally not crying.' I almost wish I *could* cry, to be honest with you. Because at least when you cry, it feels like a release, right? Like you're letting something out, like a pressure valve, and then you can take a deep breath and feel a little lighter afterward. \n\nBut with this, there's nothing to let out. It's not an emotion. It's more like... like a total absence of emotion. I just sit there on the couch and watch the sunlight move across the living room floor over the course of the afternoon, and I don't feel sad, I don't feel happy, I don't feel down... I just feel completely blank. It's just a void. So, no, I definitely wouldn't use the word sad to describe it. It's just that flat, heavy nothingness I was talking about before.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Neurasthenia is less likely given prominent loss of interest. Organic mood disorder remains as a rule-out because profound fatigue/anergia could still be secondary to a medical cause.","is_final":false}

Medical condition? No, no doctor has ever said anything like that to me. I mean, I haven't even been to a regular doctor in... gosh, it has to be at least three or four years. 

I used to go to this wonderful general practitioner, Dr. Evans, over in the West End. He was the kind of doctor who actually sat down and talked to you for twenty minutes instead of rushing you out the door. He had these little hard candies in a bowl on his desk—the strawberry ones with the soft center, you know? I loved going to him. But then he retired and moved to Florida, or maybe it was Arizona, I can't quite remember, but somewhere warm, obviously. After he left, I just... I never really got around to finding a new primary care doctor. 

I mean, I've always been generally healthy, thankfully. I've never had any major issues, no chronic conditions, nothing like that. I don't take any daily medications, except for a multivitamin when I remember to buy them, which hasn't been lately. The only time I've even seen a medical professional recently was when I went to the urgent care clinic down the street from my apartment last winter because I had this terrible sinus infection that just wouldn't go away. It was awful, I was completely congested for two weeks and my face hurt so much I couldn't even wear my glasses. The doctor there—I don't even remember his name, he was very young, looked like he was barely out of medical school—he just gave me a prescription for amoxicillin and sent me on my way. He definitely didn't mention anything about any other medical conditions or anything that would cause me to feel like this. 

And like I said, this whole... whatever this is, this total lack of interest and energy, it's so incredibly new for me. It's not like I've been dragging for years and just ignoring it. It just sort of descended on me recently. So, no, there hasn't been any doctor telling me I have a thyroid issue, or a vitamin deficiency, or... or whatever else might cause someone to just stop caring about their entire life. It's just me, sitting here, trying to figure out why my brain feels like it's been unplugged from the wall. So, yeah, no medical conditions that I know of, or that anyone has ever diagnosed me with. Just this. Just this weird, heavy blankness.
